=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
A "Snowflake Tree" is defined as a tree that can be generated by the following procedure:

- Choose positive integers x,y.
- Prepare one vertex.
- Prepare x more vertices, and connect each of them to the vertex prepared in step 2.
- For each of the x vertices prepared in step 3, attach y leaves to it.

The figure below shows a Snowflake Tree with x=4,y=2. The vertices prepared in steps 2, 3, 4 are shown in red, blue, and green, respectively.

You are given a tree T with N vertices. The vertices are numbered 1 to N, and the i-th edge (i=1,2,\dots,N-1) connects vertices u_i and v_i.
Consider deleting zero or more vertices of T and the edges adjacent to them so that the remaining graph becomes a single Snowflake Tree. Find the minimum number of vertices that must be deleted. Under the constraints of this problem, it is always possible to transform T into a Snowflake Tree.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_{N-1} v_{N-1}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 3 \leq N \leq 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq u_i < v_i \leq N
- The given graph is a tree.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

8
1 3
2 3
3 4
4 5
5 6
5 7
4 8

Sample Output 1

1

By deleting vertex 8, the given tree can be transformed into a Snowflake Tree with x=2,y=2.

Sample Input 2

3
1 2
2 3

Sample Output 2

0

The given tree is already a Snowflake Tree with x=1,y=1.

Sample Input 3

10
1 3
1 2
5 7
6 10
2 8
1 6
8 9
2 7
1 4

Sample Output 3

3


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
for i in range(N-1):
    u = int(data[1+2*i])-1; v = int(data[2+2*i])-1

- deg = [len(adj[i]) for i in range(N)]
- max_best = 0
for v in range(N):
    # If deg[v] == 0? N>=3 no.
    # Build neighbor degrees. Could avoid list for leaves? But need.
    arr = [deg[u] for u in adj[v]]
    arr.sort(reverse=True)
    best = 0
    # Since arr sorted descending, break when d < 2.
    for i, d in enumerate(arr, 1):
        if d < 2:
            break
        val = i * d
        if val > best:
            best = val
    if best > max_best:
        max_best = best

Now, let's think about if the final answer should be code only, no explanation. The user explicitly: "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be:
```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    n = data[0]
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = data[idx]-1
        v = data[idx+1]-1
        idx += 2
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    deg = [len(adj[i]) for i in range(n)]
    max_best = 0
    for v in range(n):
        arr = [deg[u] for u in adj[v]]
        arr.sort(reverse=True)
        best = 0
        for i, d in enumerate(arr, 1):
            if d < 2:
                break
            val = i * d
            if val > best:
                best = val
        if best > max_best:
            max_best = best
    print(n - 1 - max_best)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "delete zero or more vertices of T and the edges adjacent to them" - If we delete a vertex, all edges adjacent to it are deleted. If a kept leaf had an edge to a deleted vertex, that edge is deleted, so leaf degree in remaining graph is 1. Good.

That would be a cycle of length? If b connected to l1,l2 and l1-l2 edge, triangle. impossible. Good.

Good.

Thus construction always valid.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "delete zero or more vertices of T and the edges adjacent to them" - If we delete a vertex, all edges adjacent to it are deleted. If a kept leaf had an edge to a deleted vertex, that edge is deleted, so leaf degree in remaining graph is 1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible Snowflake Tree where the center c has degree x but some internal node b has original degree greater than y+1, and one of its remaining neighbors is a leaf that is also adjacent to a deleted vertex w, and w is adjacent to another selected internal b'? Then l-b-c-b'-w-l? Edges l-b, b-c, c-b', b'-w, w-l cycle length5. impossible. So no. Thus tree structure ensures no problematic adjacencies.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    n = data[0]

    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u = data[idx] - 1
        v = data[idx + 1] - 1
        idx += 2
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)

    deg = [len(adj[i]) for i in range(n)]

    max_best = 0

    for v in range(n):
        arr = [deg[u] for u in adj[v]]
        arr.sort(reverse=True)

        best = 0
        for i, d in enumerate(arr, 1):
            if d < 2:
                break
            val = i * d
            if val > best:
                best = val

        if best > max_best:
            max_best = best

    print(n - 1 - max_best)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
